**Variants:** *with a view toward* and *with an eye to* are American alternatives; *with a view to* is the standard drafting form. British writers also use *with a view towards*.
**Contrast:** Means "with the aim of." Against *in order to*, which takes a bare infinitive (*in order to reduce costs*), *with a view to* takes a gerund (*with a view to reducing costs*). Mixing the two produces the classic error.

States the purpose behind an action that is only a first step toward it. It implies the goal is intended but not yet achieved, which is why it is so common in negotiation and planning language.

1. The parties met *with a view to* settling the dispute.
2. The land was purchased *with a view to* future expansion.
3. They reorganized the department *with a view to* cutting overhead.
4. The bill was drafted *with a view to* surviving a court challenge.
5. She took the position *with a view to* eventually running the program.

**See also:** [for the sake of](#for-the-sake-of), [in search of](#in-search-of), [with respect to](#with-respect-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### with reference to

**Pronunciation:** /wɪθ ˈrɛfrəns tu/ &middot; weak /wəθ ˈrɛfrəns tə/ &middot; *with REF-runss too*
**Русский:** касательно, со ссылкой на (письмо); тех. относительно (точки отсчёта)
**Type:** complex &middot; **Register:** formal; correspondence and technical writing
**Complement:** noun phrase, gerund, or a nominal *wh*-clause (*with reference to what you raised on Tuesday*). **Never a *that*-clause**, and — the *to* being a preposition — **never a bare infinitive**.
**Pattern:** *With reference to* [noun phrase], [clause]. &middot; [clause] *with reference to* [noun phrase].
**Contrast:** The letter-opening member of the *with ___ to* group: sense 1 points back at a specific document or previous communication, where *with regard to* and *with respect to* point at a topic generally. Sense 2 is the technical one and is the reason the phrase survives at all — *measured with reference to sea level* means "using it as the baseline," which *with regard to* cannot mean. Note also ***with references to* is an error** in the topic sense, exactly parallel to *with regards to*.

**1. Concerning; further to (a document or previous message).** *With reference to* your letter of April 2, the shipment left on Monday.
**2. Measured or defined by taking something as the baseline (technical).** All elevations are given *with reference to* mean sea level.

1. *With reference to* your letter of April 2, the shipment left on Monday.
2. *With reference to* the matter we discussed, I have spoken to counsel.
3. *With reference to* what you raised on Tuesday, the committee has no position yet.
4. All elevations are given *with reference to* mean sea level.
5. The clause must be read *with reference to* the definitions in Section 1.
6. Velocity is meaningless except *with reference to* some frame.
7. The tolerances are specified *with reference to* the finished surface, not the blank.

**See also:** [with regard to](#with-regard-to), [with respect to](#with-respect-to), [further to](#further-to), [relative to](#relative-to), [regarding](12-marginal-participial.md#regarding)

### with regard to

**Pronunciation:** /wɪθ rɪˈɡɑrd tu/ &middot; weak /wəθ rɪˈɡɑrd tə/ &middot; *with ruh-GARD too*
**Русский:** относительно, что касается — офиц. замена about; форма with regards to ошибочна
**Type:** complex &middot; **Register:** formal
**Complement:** noun phrase, gerund, or a nominal *wh*-clause (*with regard to what the statute requires*). **Never a *that*-clause**, and — the *to* being a preposition — **never a bare infinitive**: *with regard to hiring*, not *with regard to hire*.
**Pattern:** *With regard to* [noun phrase], [clause]. &middot; [clause] *with regard to* [noun phrase].
**Variants:** *in regard to* and *as regards* are equally standard; *regarding* is the one-word form.
**Contrast:** ***With regards to* is an error.** *Regards* with an *s* means greetings (*give my regards to your mother*), and it belongs at the bottom of a letter, not in the middle of a sentence. The singular *regard* is the noun meaning "attention, consideration." The same rule kills *in regards to* and *as regards to*. Note also that *as regards* takes no *to* at all.

Introduces the topic the statement is about. It is the formal register's *about*, and it is nearly always compressible — *with regard to the budget* is usually just *on the budget* or *about the budget*.

1. *With regard to* the budget, no decision has been made.
2. The report says nothing *with regard to* enforcement.
3. *With regard to* your second question, the deadline has not changed.
4. Policies differ sharply *with regard to* part-time employees.
5. She has been careless *with regard to* deadlines all semester.

**See also:** [with respect to](#with-respect-to), [as regards](#as-regards), [in relation to](#in-relation-to), [as to](#as-to), [regarding](12-marginal-participial.md#regarding)

### with respect to

**Pronunciation:** /wɪθ rɪˈspɛkt tu/ &middot; weak /wəθ rɪˈspɛkt tə/ &middot; *with ruh-SPEKT too*
**Русский:** в отношении, касательно; мат. по (переменной): дифференцировать по времени
**Type:** complex &middot; **Register:** formal; the standard American form in legal, technical, and academic prose
**Complement:** noun phrase, gerund, or a nominal *wh*-clause (*with respect to whether the filing was timely*). **Never a *that*-clause**, and — the *to* being a preposition — **never a bare infinitive**: *with respect to filing*, not *with respect to file*.
**Pattern:** *With respect to* [noun phrase], [clause]. &middot; [clause] *with respect to* [noun phrase].
**Variants:** Abbreviated *w.r.t.* in mathematics and engineering. *In respect of* is the British counterpart; *in respect to* is a less common American variant.
**Contrast:** Interchangeable with *with regard to* as a topic marker, but *with respect to* also has a technical sense that *with regard to* lacks: *differentiate with respect to x*, *symmetric with respect to the origin* — "taking x as the variable or reference." Note that the phrase has nothing to do with deference; *with all due respect* is a different expression entirely. And as with *with regard to*, ***with respects to* is an error** — the plural belongs only in *pay one's respects*.

**1. On the subject of; as far as X is concerned (topic marker).** *With respect to* the second charge, the jury could not agree.
**2. Taking X as the variable, baseline, or axis (technical).** Differentiate the function *with respect to* time.

1. *With respect to* the second charge, the jury could not agree.
2. The statute is silent *with respect to* electronic filings.
3. *With respect to* scheduling, please copy the front office on everything.
4. The parties remain apart *with respect to* whether the notice was timely.
5. Differentiate the function *with respect to* time.
6. The two designs are identical *with respect to* power draw.
7. The curve is symmetric *with respect to* the origin.

**See also:** [with regard to](#with-regard-to), [in respect of](#in-respect-of), [relative to](#relative-to), [as to](#as-to), [in terms of](#in-terms-of)

### with the exception of

**Pronunciation:** /wɪð ði ɪkˈsɛpʃən əv/ &middot; weak /wəð ði ɪkˈsɛpʃən ə/ &middot; *with thee ik-SEP-shun uhv*
**Русский:** за исключением — вычитает пункт из утверждения об обо всех
**Type:** complex &middot; **Register:** formal
**Complement:** noun phrase, or a gerund at a pinch (*with the exception of having to repaint*). **Never a *that*-clause, never a *wh*-clause** — the clause-taking form is the conjunction *except that*.
**Pattern:** *With the exception of* [noun phrase], [clause]. &middot; [clause] *with the exception of* [noun phrase].
**Contrast:** The heaviest member of the exception family. *Except for*, *apart from*, *aside from*, and bare *except* all do the same work in fewer words. It is worth the length only when the exception needs to be foregrounded, or in drafting where *except* alone might be read as attaching to the wrong element.

Subtracts a named item from an otherwise universal claim. The claim must be a genuine generalization; subtracting from a specific statement produces nonsense.

1. *With the exception of* Tuesday, the whole week is booked.
2. Every state, *with the exception of* Nebraska, has a bicameral legislature.
3. The building was undamaged, *with the exception of* two broken windows.
4. All fees are refundable *with the exception of* the application charge.
5. *With the exception of* one abstention, the vote was unanimous.

**See also:** [except for](#except-for), [apart from](#apart-from), [aside from](#aside-from), [save for](#save-for), [other than](#other-than), [excepting](12-marginal-participial.md#excepting)

### with the help of

**Pronunciation:** /wɪð ðə ˈhɛlp əv/ &middot; weak /wəð ðə ˈhɛl pə/ &middot; *with thuh HELP uhv*
**Русский:** с помощью — вклад помощника; ответственность остаётся за субъектом
**Type:** complex &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase naming a person, body, or instrument; a gerund is possible but uncommon. **Never a *that*-clause.** A free relative is fine (*with the help of whoever happened to be around*).
**Pattern:** [clause] *with the help of* [noun phrase]. &middot; *With the help of* [noun phrase], [clause].
**Variants:** *with the aid of* is the formal twin and is preferred for instruments (*with the aid of a magnifying glass*); *with the assistance of* is the most formal of the three; *with help from* is the looser conversational version. **The *of* is not optional, and *with the help from* is an error.**
**Contrast:** Against [*by means of*](#by-means-of), which names a pure instrument, *with the help of* usually names a collaborator and always implies that the subject did the work too — the helper assisted, and did not do it alone. That is also the difference from [*thanks to*](#thanks-to), which hands the credit over entirely: *We finished thanks to Maria* makes her contribution decisive, *We finished with the help of Maria* makes it partial.

Names a contributor to an effort the subject remains responsible for. The main clause keeps the agency; the phrase only shares it.

1. *With the help of* two neighbors, they had the roof patched by dark.
2. She finished the application *with the help of* a legal aid clinic.
3. The map was reconstructed *with the help of* nineteenth-century tax records.
4. *With the help of* a state grant, the library stayed open on Sundays.
5. He got through the first year *with the help of* whoever would tutor him for free.

**See also:** [by means of](#by-means-of), [thanks to](#thanks-to), [courtesy of](#courtesy-of), [together with](#together-with), [with the aid of](07-agent-instrument-manner.md#with-the-aid-of)

## The *___ of* and *___ from* family

Units that close in *of* or *from* and are built on something other than *in*: usually a bare adverb, adjective, or noun, and in a few cases a small *at the ___ of*, *on the ___ of*, *to the ___ of*, or *under the ___ of* frame of their own — one of which, *on the lookout for*, closes with *for* rather than *of*. This is the group where the frozen/literal distinction bites hardest, because the first word usually leads an independent life as an adverb: *ahead* and *out* and *inside* and *apart* all stand alone without an object, and adding *of* or *from* is precisely what turns them into prepositions. **The test is the object**: *He walked out* is an adverb; *He walked out of the room* is a preposition with a complement. Two members of the group carry live usage disputes — *inside of* and *outside of*, where the *of* is optional and often edited out, and *regardless of*, whose deformation *irregardless* is the most notorious nonstandard word in American English.

### a lot of

**Pronunciation:** /ə ˈlɑt əv/ &middot; weak /ə ˈlɑɾ ə/ — the *t* flaps and *of* reduces to a bare schwa, giving the ordinary spoken *uh-LAH-duh* &middot; *uh LOT uhv*
**Русский:** много, масса — квантификатор при существительном; формально определитель, не предлог
**Type:** complex quantifier — a quantifying determinative rather than a true preposition &middot; **Register:** *a lot of* and *lots of* informal to neutral; *a number of* and *a great deal of* formal
**Complement:** **a noun phrase only.** Never a *that*-clause, and no gerund as head (*a lot of running* is a noun). The real division inside the family is countability: *a lot of*, *lots of*, *plenty of*, *a bunch of*, and *a ton of* take **either** count plurals or mass nouns (*a lot of chairs*, *a lot of furniture*); *a number of* and *a couple of* take **count plurals only** (never *a number of furniture*); *a bit of* and *a great deal of* take **mass nouns only** (never *a bit of chairs*).
**Pattern:** *a lot of* [plural or mass noun] + [verb agreeing with that noun] &middot; *a number of* [plural noun] + [**plural** verb] &middot; *the number of* [plural noun] + [**singular** verb]
**Variants:** *lots of*, *plenty of*, *a bunch of*, *a couple of*, *a number of*, *a bit of*, *a great deal of*, *a ton of* — one frame, differing in register and in what each will count.
**Contrast:** **The agreement trap is why this belongs in a book about frozen phrases.** The verb agrees with the noun **after** *of*, never with *lot* or *number*: *A lot of the money **is** missing* but *A lot of the files **are** missing*. And in the *number* pair the article decides everything: ***A** number of students **are** waiting* (many students) versus ***The** number of students **is** rising* (one number). Against a true preposition such as [*out of*](#out-of), notice that the whole unit is the subject's determiner, not a modifier attached to it, which is why it governs no agreement of its own. **Bare *a lot* is a degree adverb and takes no object** — *She travels a lot*, *Thanks a lot* — and there it is not this item at all. Finally, *a lot* is two words; *alot* is not a word.

Quantifies the noun that follows. It is listed here because it wears the *___ of* costume of the complex prepositions and is frozen in exactly the same way — *a lots of*, *a many of*, and bare *lot of* are all impossible — while grammatically being a determiner, and because the subject–verb agreement it governs is the reason to learn it as a unit.

1. *A lot of* the money set aside for repairs is still unspent.
2. *A lot of* people showed up who had never voted in a primary before.
3. There's *plenty of* time; the train doesn't leave until nine.
4. *A number of* residents are suing the county over the assessment.
5. *The number of* residents suing the county is now up to forty.
6. It took *a bit of* persuading and *a couple of* phone calls.
7. He talks *a lot* — and that bare *a lot* is an adverb, not this quantifier.

**See also:** [kind of](#kind-of), [out of](#out-of), [in excess of](#in-excess-of), [short of](#short-of), [a matter of](07-agent-instrument-manner.md#a-matter-of), [the better part of](07-agent-instrument-manner.md#the-better-part-of)

### ahead of

**Pronunciation:** /əˈhɛd əv/ &middot; connected speech /əˈhɛ də/ &middot; *uh-HED uhv*
**Русский:** впереди (в очереди); раньше, до (срока); опережая (прогноз, соперника)
**Type:** complex &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or objective-case pronoun. **Never a *that*-clause, never a *wh*-clause.** Sense 2 also takes a gerund (*ahead of leaving*), which sense 1 does not.
**Pattern:** [clause] *ahead of* [noun phrase]. &middot; *Ahead of* [noun phrase], [clause]. &middot; *well/far/just ahead of* [noun phrase]
**Contrast:** Against *in front of*, sense 1 implies a shared line of travel or a queue — *the car ahead of us* is going the same way, *the car in front of us* may be parked crosswise. Against *before*, sense 2 is the journalistic word for anticipation: *ahead of the vote* rather than *before the vote*, with an implication of preparation. Bare *ahead* with no object is an adverb, not this preposition: *Go on ahead.*

**1. In front of, in a line or sequence.** There were six people *ahead of* us.
**2. Earlier than; in anticipation of.** The bill was filed *ahead of* Friday's deadline.
**3. Outperforming; further along than.** She finished the year *ahead of* every projection.

1. There were six people *ahead of* us at the counter.
2. The lead car pulled two lengths *ahead of* the field.
3. Markets were jittery *ahead of* the jobs report.
4. *Ahead of* the storm, the county opened four shelters.
5. Construction is running three weeks *ahead of* schedule.
6. She finished the quarter well *ahead of* every internal projection.
7. He is years *ahead of* his classmates in math.

**See also:** [in front of](#in-front-of), [prior to](#prior-to), [in advance of](#in-advance-of), [next to](#next-to), [up to](#up-to), [before](05-time.md#before)

### apart from

**Pronunciation:** /əˈpɑrt frʌm/ &middot; weak /əˈpɑrt frəm/ &middot; *uh-PART frum*
**Русский:** кроме, не считая; тж. помимо, вдобавок к — какой смысл, решает контекст
**Type:** complex &middot; **Register:** neutral; more common in British English than in American, where *aside from* is preferred
**Complement:** noun phrase, gerund (*apart from being expensive*), objective-case pronoun (*apart from him*), or a nominal *wh*-clause (*apart from what he told me*). **Never a *that*-clause.**
**Pattern:** *Apart from* [noun phrase / verb-ing], [clause]. &middot; [clause] *apart from* [noun phrase].
**Contrast:** **The same phrase means two opposite things** — subtraction (sense 1) and addition (sense 2) — and only context tells them apart: *Apart from the noise, it's a good apartment* subtracts; *Apart from the noise, there's the rent* adds. American writers use *aside from* far more often for both. Note the literal reading too: *He stood apart from the group* is adverb *apart* + preposition *from*, not this unit.

**1. Except for; leaving aside.** *Apart from* one bad review, the show was a hit.
**2. In addition to; besides.** *Apart from* the salary, there's a housing allowance.

1. *Apart from* one bad review, the show was a hit.
2. I know nothing about her *apart from* her name.
3. *Apart from* being expensive, the software is genuinely hard to use.
4. *Apart from* the salary, there's a housing allowance and a car.
5. Who came, *apart from* the two of you?
6. *Apart from* that, how was the trip?

**See also:** [aside from](#aside-from), [except for](#except-for), [other than](#other-than), [in addition to](#in-addition-to), [with the exception of](#with-the-exception-of)

### aside from

**Pronunciation:** /əˈsaɪd frʌm/ &middot; weak /əˈsaɪd frəm/ &middot; *uh-SYDE frum*
**Русский:** кроме, если не считать; тж. помимо, вдобавок к — амер. соответствие apart from
**Type:** complex &middot; **Register:** neutral; **the standard American form** where British English says *apart from*
**Complement:** noun phrase, gerund, objective-case pronoun, or a nominal *wh*-clause (*aside from what you already know*). **Never a *that*-clause.**
**Pattern:** *Aside from* [noun phrase / verb-ing], [clause]. &middot; [clause] *aside from* [noun phrase].
**Contrast:** Identical in meaning and in double sense to *apart from* — it both subtracts and adds — and differs only in national preference. Against *except for*, which only subtracts, *aside from* is ambiguous, so use *except for* when the exception reading has to be unmistakable. **Bare *aside* is an adverb and takes no object** — *Step aside*, *She set the money aside*, *joking aside* — where *aside from* always requires one; the two words cannot be separated.

**1. Except for.** *Aside from* a sore knee, he came through the season fine.
**2. In addition to.** *Aside from* the fine, you lose four points on your license.

1. *Aside from* a sore knee, he came through the season fine.
2. *Aside from* Tuesday, I'm free all week.
3. There's nothing in the fridge *aside from* condiments.
4. *Aside from* the fine, you lose four points on your license.
5. *Aside from* being the cheapest option, it's also the fastest.
6. *Aside from* that, I have no complaints.

**See also:** [apart from](#apart-from), [except for](#except-for), [other than](#other-than), [with the exception of](#with-the-exception-of), [in addition to](#in-addition-to)

### at the expense of

**Pronunciation:** /æt ði ɪkˈspɛns əv/ &middot; weak /ət ði ɪkˈspɛn sə/ — *at* reduces to /ət/ &middot; *at thee ik-SPENSS uhv*
**Русский:** за счёт (чьих-то средств); тж. ценой, в ущерб (чему-то)
**Type:** complex &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** noun phrase or gerund (*at the expense of losing accuracy*). **Never a *that*-clause, never a *wh*-clause.**
**Pattern:** [clause] *at the expense of* [noun phrase / verb-ing]. &middot; [what was gained], *at the expense of* [what was given up]
**Contrast:** The two senses are told apart by the object: money and a payer give sense 1, a value or an injured party gives sense 2. **Sense 2 is the commoner one in writing and always asserts a completed trade-off** — *speed at the expense of accuracy* claims accuracy was actually lost, which is a stronger claim than *rather than* or *instead of* would make. Do not confuse it with the fixed phrase *a joke at his expense*, where the person is the target rather than the payer.

**1. Paid for by.** The trip was made *at the expense of* the foundation.
**2. With a loss or sacrifice of; to the detriment of.** The plant gained speed *at the expense of* accuracy.

1. The trip was made *at the expense of* the foundation, not the taxpayers.
2. Repairs were carried out *at the expense of* the previous owner.
3. The redesign gained speed *at the expense of* readability.
4. Growth came *at the expense of* every small grower in the valley.
5. He built the department *at the expense of* his own research.
6. They shortened the timeline *at the expense of* testing anything properly.

**See also:** [in favor of](#in-favor-of), [instead of](#instead-of), [as opposed to](#as-opposed-to), [courtesy of](#courtesy-of), [at the expense of](07-agent-instrument-manner.md#at-the-expense-of)

### at the hands of

**Pronunciation:** /æt ðə ˈhændz əv/ &middot; weak /ət ðə ˈhændz ə/ — *at* reduces to /ət/ &middot; *at thuh HANDZ uhv*
**Русский:** от рук (кого-то) — называет виновника вреда, поражения, дурного обращения
**Type:** complex &middot; **Register:** formal to neutral; news writing, history, and sports reporting
**Complement:** noun phrase or objective-case pronoun naming a person, group, or institution — **never an inanimate force**. **Never a *that*-clause, never a gerund.** A free relative naming an agent is fine (*at the hands of whoever was guarding him*).
**Pattern:** [noun of harm or defeat] *at the hands of* [person or body] &middot; [clause] *at the hands of* [person or body]
**Contrast:** An agentive phrase with a restriction built into it: **what happens at somebody's hands is always something bad.** *A beating at the hands of the guards* and *a defeat at the hands of an unranked team* are idiomatic; *a gift at the hands of her uncle* is not, and plain *by* is the neutral form for everything else. It also insists on a human or corporate agent, which is why *damage at the hands of the storm* is a misuse where *damage from the storm* is right — compare [*at the mercy of*](#at-the-mercy-of), which takes forces freely because it reports powerlessness rather than injury. Note that *hands* is invariable: there is no *at the hand of* in this sense.

Names the agent responsible for harm, defeat, or mistreatment. The main clause supplies the injury and the phrase supplies who inflicted it, which is why it cannot be used where nothing bad occurred.

1. The team suffered its worst loss of the season *at the hands of* an unranked opponent.
2. Hundreds died *at the hands of* the regime in the first month alone.
3. She described years of mistreatment *at the hands of* a former employer.
4. The manuscript met a sorry end *at the hands of* an overzealous editor.
5. The measure died *at the hands of* the very committee that drafted it.
6. He lost the title *at the hands of* a fighter nobody had heard of in March.

**See also:** [at the mercy of](#at-the-mercy-of), [on the part of](#on-the-part-of), [courtesy of](#courtesy-of), [because of](#because-of), [at the hands of](07-agent-instrument-manner.md#at-the-hands-of), [by](02-core-relational.md#by)

### at the heart of

**Pronunciation:** /ət ðə ˈhɑrt əv/ &middot; weak /ət ðə ˈhɑr ɾə/ — *at* reduces to /ət/ and the *t* of *heart* flaps into the *of* &middot; *at thuh HART uhv*
**Русский:** в основе, в сердцевине — главное, по сравнению с чем остальное второстепенно
**Type:** complex &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal; journalism, criticism, and argument
**Complement:** noun phrase, gerund (*at the heart of deciding who pays*), or a free relative (*at the heart of what the committee is fighting about*). **Never a *that*-clause.**
**Pattern:** [noun] *is at the heart of* [noun phrase] &middot; *At the heart of* [noun phrase] *is* [noun] (inverted, and very common)
**Contrast:** Figurative only: the literal anatomical string is *in the heart*, and *at the heart of the chest* is not English. Against [*in the middle of*](#in-the-middle-of), which is spatial or temporal, *at the heart of* is about **importance**, not position — *at the heart of the dispute* names the essential issue, not the midpoint. Against *at the center of*, it claims essence where *at the center of* claims mere involvement: *at the center of the scandal* is usually a person, *at the heart of the scandal* is usually the issue. The inverted pattern, *At the heart of the bill is a single sentence*, is a stylistic staple and is worth learning as a unit.

Identifies the essential element of something larger. It is an argumentative move as much as a description: to put X *at the heart of* Y is to claim that everything else about Y is secondary.

1. *At the heart of* the dispute is a single ambiguous sentence in the lease.
2. Water rights are *at the heart of* nearly every fight in the basin.
3. *At the heart of* the novel is a question the narrator never answers.
4. The audit found a filing error *at the heart of* the whole discrepancy.
5. *At the heart of* what the committee is fighting about is who signs the checks.
6. Trust, or its absence, sits *at the heart of* every one of these negotiations.

**See also:** [in the middle of](#in-the-middle-of), [in the midst of](#in-the-midst-of), [on the basis of](#on-the-basis-of), [in terms of](#in-terms-of), [amid](03-place-position.md#amid)

### at the mercy of

**Pronunciation:** /æt ðə ˈmɜrsi əv/ &middot; weak /ət ðə ˈmɜrsi ə/ — *at* reduces to /ət/ &middot; *at thuh MUR-see uhv*
**Русский:** во власти, на милость — субъект не может этому противостоять
**Type:** complex &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** noun phrase naming a person, body, or uncontrollable force. **Never a *that*-clause, never a gerund.** A free relative is fine and is common (*at the mercy of whatever the wholesalers decide to pay*).
**Pattern:** *be at the mercy of* [noun phrase] &middot; *leave someone at the mercy of* [noun phrase] &middot; *entirely/completely/utterly at the mercy of* [noun phrase]
**Contrast:** It asserts **powerlessness, not cruelty** — *at the mercy of the weather* accuses the weather of nothing, and the phrase is fully idiomatic with forces that have no intentions at all. That is what separates it from [*at the hands of*](#at-the-hands-of), which requires an agent and requires that harm actually occurred: you are *at the mercy of* a landlord before anything happens and *at the hands of* one only after it does. The related expressions *have mercy on* and *without mercy* are ordinary noun uses and not this preposition.

Places the subject under something it cannot control. What follows may be a person with power or a force with none — the phrase cares only that the subject cannot resist it.

1. Small growers are *at the mercy of* whatever the wholesalers decide to pay.
2. The lifeboat was *at the mercy of* the current for nine hours.
3. Without the subsidy, the clinic is *at the mercy of* private donors.
4. The schedule leaves the whole project *at the mercy of* a single supplier.
5. Renters here are completely *at the mercy of* a market with no vacancies.
6. He refused to be *at the mercy of* a committee that met twice a year.

**See also:** [at the hands of](#at-the-hands-of), [subject to](#subject-to), [depending on](#depending-on), [up against](#up-against), [in the face of](#in-the-face-of)

### at the option of

**Pronunciation:** /ət ði ˈɑpʃən əv/ &middot; weak /ət ði ˈɑpʃə nə/ &middot; *at thee OP-shun uhv*
**Русский:** по усмотрению, по выбору — юр.; кому принадлежит право выбора
**Type:** complex &middot; **Register:** formal; contracts, bond indentures, insurance policies, and leases
**Complement:** noun phrase naming the party who holds the choice. **Never a *that*-clause and never a gerund.**
**Pattern:** [right or action] *at the option of* [noun phrase] &middot; *exercisable at the option of* [noun phrase] &middot; *at the option of the holder / the issuer / the buyer*
**Contrast:** The complement is **whoever gets to choose**, not what is chosen — *redeemable at the option of the issuer* means the issuer decides, and for a bondholder that single phrase is the difference between a callable bond and a puttable one. Against [*at the pleasure of*](#at-the-pleasure-of), which is about tenure in office and implies removal without cause, this one assigns a contractual election. Against plain *if X chooses*, it is the drafting form and is genuinely clearer in a document with several parties in play.

Assigns a choice to a named party. In finance it is the phrase that tells you who holds the valuable side of an option, which is why it repays slow reading.

1. The notes are redeemable *at the option of* the issuer after three years.
2. The lease may be extended for five years *at the option of* the tenant.
3. Payment is by check or wire transfer, *at the option of* the payer.
4. Arbitration is available *at the option of* the consumer only.
5. *At the option of* the buyer, the closing may be moved up by ten days.
6. Which party holds the option decides what it is worth, so read *at the option of* carefully.

**See also:** [at the pleasure of](#at-the-pleasure-of), [subject to](#subject-to), [under the terms of](#under-the-terms-of), [depending on](#depending-on), [at the behest of](07-agent-instrument-manner.md#at-the-behest-of)

### at the pleasure of

**Pronunciation:** /ət ðə ˈplɛʒər əv/ &middot; weak /ət ðə ˈplɛʒə rə/ &middot; *at thuh PLEZH-ur uhv*
**Русский:** по усмотрению (назначившего) — должность держится, пока угодно этой власти
**Type:** complex &middot; **Register:** formal; constitutional and employment law, and government service generally
**Complement:** noun phrase naming the appointing authority — a president, a governor, a board, a sheriff. **Never a *that*-clause and never a gerund.**
**Pattern:** *serve at the pleasure of* [noun phrase] &middot; *hold office at the pleasure of* [noun phrase] &middot; *removable at the pleasure of* [noun phrase]
**Contrast:** *Pleasure* here means **will**, not enjoyment, and the phrase is a statement about **tenure**: to serve *at the pleasure of* the president is to be removable at any time, for any reason or for none. That is the entire content of the expression, and it is why every American cabinet secretary is described this way. Against [*at the option of*](#at-the-option-of), which assigns a contractual choice, this one assigns the power to dismiss. Against *at will*, the employment-law term for the same arrangement between private parties, *at the pleasure of* is the public-office version. The British ancestor, *during Her Majesty's pleasure*, is a sentencing formula and means something else entirely.

States that a position is held only so long as the named authority wishes. It is one of the few phrases in American public life whose legal meaning is exactly its literal one.

1. Cabinet secretaries serve *at the pleasure of* the president.
2. The city manager holds the post *at the pleasure of* the council.
3. Deputies are appointed by and serve *at the pleasure of* the sheriff.
4. She was reminded, not gently, that she served *at the pleasure of* the board.
5. Members of the commission serve fixed terms and are not removable *at the pleasure of* the governor.
6. *Pleasure* here means will, not enjoyment: the phrase is about who can fire you.

**See also:** [at the option of](#at-the-option-of), [at the mercy of](#at-the-mercy-of), [under the authority of](#under-the-authority-of), [subject to](#subject-to), [at the behest of](07-agent-instrument-manner.md#at-the-behest-of)

### away from

**Pronunciation:** /əˈweɪ frʌm/ &middot; weak /əˈweɪ frəm/ &middot; *uh-WAY frum*
**Русский:** прочь от, в сторону от; вдали от, вне (общества кого-то); на расстоянии от
**Type:** complex &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or objective-case pronoun; **never a gerund and never a *that*-clause**. A free relative is fine (*away from what she had grown up with*). Sense 3 takes a measure phrase before it (*two blocks away from*).
**Pattern:** [clause] *away from* [noun phrase] &middot; [measure] *away from* [noun phrase] &middot; *Away from* [noun phrase], [clause].
**Contrast:** *Away* alone is an adverb (*She's away this week*); the preposition needs *from* and an object. Against bare *from*, *away from* stresses separation and increasing distance rather than origin: *He came from Detroit* names a source, *He moved away from Detroit* describes a departure.

**1. Moving so as to increase distance.** She backed slowly *away from* the dog.
**2. Separated from; not in the company of.** He is different *away from* his family.
**3. At a stated distance or remove (with a measure phrase).** The school is two blocks *away from* here.

1. She backed slowly *away from* the dog.
2. Keep the heater *away from* the curtains.
3. He is a completely different person *away from* the office.
4. *Away from* the tourist streets, the city is quiet.
5. The nearest hospital is forty minutes *away from* the site.
6. We're one win *away from* the playoffs.
7. She has spent ten years moving *away from* what she was raised to believe.

**See also:** [apart from](#apart-from), [out of](#out-of), [close to](#close-to), [from](02-core-relational.md#from)

### because of

**Pronunciation:** /bɪˈkɔz əv/ or /bɪˈkʌz əv/ &middot; casual /bɪˈkʌzə/, /ˈkʌzə/ (*'cause of*) &middot; *bih-KUZ uhv*
**Русский:** из-за, по причине — называет причину события (обстоятельство при глаголе)
**Type:** complex &middot; **Register:** neutral — the default causal preposition of American English
**Complement:** noun phrase, gerund (*because of being late*), or a nominal *wh*-clause (*because of who he knows*, *because of what the inspector found*). **Never a *that*-clause** — *because* alone is the conjunction that takes a clause (*because it rained*), and *because of it rained* is impossible.
**Pattern:** [clause] *because of* [noun phrase]. &middot; *Because of* [noun phrase], [clause].
**Contrast:** ***Because* + clause versus *because of* + noun phrase** is the cleanest preposition/conjunction pair in English: *We stayed home because it rained* / *We stayed home because of the rain*. Against *due to*, *because of* is adverbial and never disputed; against *owing to* and *on account of*, it is the plain, unmarked choice. The internet-era *because noun* (*because science*) is a deliberate joke and is not standard.

Names the cause of the event in the main clause. It modifies the verb or the whole clause, which is exactly what distinguishes it from *due to* in the traditional rule.

1. The flight was canceled *because of* fog.
2. *Because of* the strike, deliveries are running two days late.
3. She missed the deadline *because of* a family emergency.
4. He got the job *because of* who he knows, not what he knows.
5. *Because of* being out of the country, he never received the notice.
6. Attendance dropped *because of* the parking situation.

**See also:** [due to](#due-to), [owing to](#owing-to), [on account of](#on-account-of), [as a result of](#as-a-result-of), [thanks to](#thanks-to), [in view of](#in-view-of)

### courtesy of

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkɜrtəsi əv/ &middot; connected speech /ˈkɜrtəsi ə/ &middot; *KUR-tuh-see uhv*
**Русский:** любезно предоставлено (кем-то) — в титрах; тж. ирон. «благодаря» неприятной причине
**Type:** complex &middot; **Register:** neutral in sense 1; informal and usually ironic in sense 2
**Complement:** noun phrase, almost always naming a person, body, or organization; **never a gerund and never a *that*-clause**. A free relative naming an agent is idiomatic in sense 2 (*courtesy of whoever left the gate open*).
**Pattern:** [noun phrase], *courtesy of* [noun phrase]. &middot; [clause] *courtesy of* [noun phrase].
**Variants:** *by courtesy of* is the older full form and survives in formal credit lines; American English has generally dropped the *by*.
**Contrast:** Sense 1 is an attribution formula that presumes permission was granted — *photograph courtesy of the state archive*. Sense 2 has drifted into sarcasm and thanks nobody: *a two-hour delay, courtesy of the airline*. Against *thanks to*, which is also ironizable, *courtesy of* keeps a note of formal crediting even when the tone is bitter.

**1. Given, lent, or supplied by (a credit line).** All images *courtesy of* the Newberry Library.
**2. As the result of (often unwelcome).** We got a flooded basement, *courtesy of* the city's storm drains.

1. All photographs are *courtesy of* the state historical society.
2. The venue was donated *courtesy of* a local foundation.
3. Dinner is *courtesy of* the hotel, since they lost our reservation.
4. We got a flooded basement, *courtesy of* the city's storm drains.
5. Another three-hour delay, *courtesy of* the airline's scheduling.
6. He has a permanent limp, *courtesy of* one bad tackle in 1998.
7. The whole hallway smells like smoke, *courtesy of* whoever propped the fire door open.

**See also:** [thanks to](#thanks-to), [on behalf of](#on-behalf-of), [by way of](#by-way-of), [because of](#because-of)

### devoid of

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈvɔɪd əv/ &middot; connected speech /dɪˈvɔɪ də/ &middot; *dih-VOYD uhv*
**Русский:** лишённый (качества) — книжн.; полное отсутствие, не допускает смягчения
**Type:** complex (adjective + fixed preposition) &middot; **Register:** formal, literary
**Complement:** noun phrase only, and characteristically an abstract mass noun (*devoid of humor*, *devoid of merit*). ✗ *Never a *that*-clause, never a *wh*-clause, never a gerund.** ✗ *It cannot stand without *of*** — there is no *the room was devoid*.
**Pattern:** *be devoid of* [noun phrase] &middot; [noun] *devoid of* [noun phrase] &middot; *entirely/utterly/wholly devoid of* [noun phrase]
**Contrast:** Stronger and more literary than *without* or *lacking*: *devoid of* asserts total absence, not mere shortage. Against *void of*, which is archaic outside legal boilerplate, *devoid of* is the living form. Against *free of*, which is positive (*free of charge*), *devoid of* is negative or critical.

Declares a complete absence of some quality. Because it is absolute, it resists hedging — *somewhat devoid of* is a solecism — but it takes intensifiers freely.

1. The argument is utterly *devoid of* evidence.
2. His prose is elegant but entirely *devoid of* warmth.
3. The room was *devoid of* furniture except for a folding chair.
4. The claim is *devoid of* any legal merit.
5. She delivered the line *devoid of* inflection.

**See also:** [void of](#void-of), [short of](#short-of), [in need of](#in-need-of), [exclusive of](#exclusive-of), [in the absence of](#in-the-absence-of)

### different from

**Pronunciation:** /ˈdɪfrənt frʌm/ &middot; also /ˈdɪfərənt/ &middot; weak /ˈdɪfrənt frəm/ &middot; *DIF-runt frum*
**Русский:** отличный от, не такой как — нормативная американская форма (не different than)
**Type:** complex (adjective + fixed preposition) &middot; **Register:** neutral — ***different from* is the American standard**
**Complement:** noun phrase, objective-case pronoun (*different from me*, never *different from I*), gerund (*different from working nights*), or a free relative (*different from what I expected*). **Never a *that*-clause.**
**Pattern:** [X] *is different from* [Y] &middot; *very / completely / no different from* [noun phrase] &middot; [noun] *different from* [noun phrase]
**Variants:** ***Different than*** is standard American English **before a clause**, where it saves a heavy *from what*: *The result was different than I expected* is accepted everywhere, and *different from what I expected* is the longer, unimpeachable form. **Before a plain noun phrase — *different than me*, *different than the others* — it is the form editors change**, and *from* is what to write. ***Different to*** is British and Australian and does not belong in American prose.
**Contrast:** **The whole rule in one line: *from* before a noun, *from what* or *than* before a clause, never *to* in American writing.** The reason *from* is the default is the verb behind the adjective — one thing *differs from* another, and nobody writes *differs than*. Against [*similar to*](#similar-to), its exact opposite, the pair is worth memorizing together, because English hands the two comparison adjectives opposite prepositions and gives no reason for it. Against *unlike*, which is a one-word preposition doing the same job, *different from* is predicative where *unlike* is not: *Unlike her sister, she plays* cannot be rewritten as *Different from her sister, she plays*.

States non-identity between two things. It is freely gradable — *slightly different from*, *no different from*, *completely different from* — which is what keeps *different* a live adjective rather than a frozen particle.

1. Her second album is completely *different from* the first.
2. Teaching adults is *different from* teaching eight-year-olds.
3. The rules are *different from* the ones we trained on.
4. The result was *different from* what anyone had predicted.
5. His account is not materially *different from* hers.
6. This model is no *different from* last year's except for the badge.
7. Nothing about the second inspection was *different from* the first.

**See also:** [similar to](#similar-to), [other than](#other-than), [as opposed to](#as-opposed-to), [in contrast to](#in-contrast-to), [different from](09-comparison-opposition.md#different-from), [unlike](07-agent-instrument-manner.md#unlike)

### exclusive of

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˈsklusɪv əv/ &middot; connected speech /ɪkˈsklusɪ və/ &middot; *ik-SKLOO-siv uhv*
**Русский:** не считая, без учёта — офиц.; исключает пункт из названной суммы
**Type:** complex &middot; **Register:** formal; commercial, accounting, and legal
**Complement:** noun phrase only. **Never a *that*-clause, never a *wh*-clause, never a gerund.**
**Pattern:** [statement of amount] *exclusive of* [noun phrase]
**Contrast:** The direct opposite of [*inclusive of*](08-accompaniment-exception.md#inclusive-of), and the pair is the standard way to fix the boundaries of a quoted figure: *$400 exclusive of tax* versus *$400 inclusive of tax*. Against *except for* and *apart from*, this one is confined to quantities and lists — you cannot say *exclusive of that, I enjoyed it*.

Excludes an item from a stated total. It is a pricing and drafting word, and outside those contexts *not including* is better.

1. The rate is $180 a night *exclusive of* tax and resort fees.
2. The team has eleven employees *exclusive of* contractors.
3. Damages are capped at $50,000 *exclusive of* interest and costs.
4. Delivery takes four business days *exclusive of* weekends.
5. The budget totals $2.1 million *exclusive of* capital expenditures.

**See also:** [inclusive of](08-accompaniment-exception.md#inclusive-of), [with the exception of](#with-the-exception-of), [except for](#except-for), [short of](#short-of), [devoid of](#devoid-of)

### far from

**Pronunciation:** /ˈfɑr frʌm/ &middot; weak /ˈfɑr frəm/ &middot; *FAR frum*
**Русский:** далеко от (о расстоянии); тж. отнюдь не, вовсе не (far from finished — далеко не закончен)
**Type:** complex &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase in sense 1; **an adjective, a noun, or a gerund** in sense 2 (*far from perfect*, *far from a solution*, *far from being finished*); a free relative in either (*far from what we were promised*). **Never a *that*-clause.**
**Pattern:** [clause] *far from* [noun phrase] &middot; *be far from* [adjective / noun / verb-ing] &middot; *Far from* [noun phrase / verb-ing], [clause].
**Contrast:** **Sense 2 is a negator, not a distance word.** *Far from perfect* means "not perfect at all," and the fronted form — *Far from helping, the new rule made things worse* — denies the first idea and asserts its opposite, so a learner who reads it as a comparison gets the sentence exactly backwards. Against [*away from*](#away-from), which describes motion or growing separation, *far from* states a distance already in place: you move *away from* the coast and then live *far from* it. **Bare *far* is an adverb and takes no object** (*We didn't go far*, *far too expensive*), and the fixed reply *far from it*, meaning "not at all," takes the invariable pronoun and nothing else.

**1. At a great distance from (literal).** They live *far from* the nearest hospital.
**2. Not at all; the opposite of (with an adjective, noun, or gerund).** The building is *far from* finished.

1. They live *far from* the nearest hospital.
2. *Far from* the tourist streets, the prices drop by half.
3. The building is *far from* finished.
4. The evidence is *far from* conclusive.
5. *Far from* helping, the new rule made the backlog worse.
6. Her explanation was *far from* what the committee had been told.
7. "Was it easy?" "*Far from* it."

**See also:** [away from](#away-from), [close to](#close-to), [short of](#short-of), [other than](#other-than), [near](03-place-position.md#near)

### inside of

**Pronunciation:** /ɪnˈsaɪd əv/ &middot; connected speech /ɪnˈsaɪ də/ &middot; *in-SYDE uhv*
**Русский:** внутри (of необязательно); тж. амер. менее чем за (два часа)
**Type:** complex &middot; **Register:** informal to neutral; **chiefly American**
**Complement:** noun phrase only. **Never a *that*-clause, never a *wh*-clause, never a gerund.** Sense 2 requires a duration noun (*inside of an hour*).
**Pattern:** [clause] *inside of* [noun phrase] &middot; *inside of* [duration]
**Contrast:** **In the spatial sense the *of* is optional and usually edited out** — *inside the house* is preferred to *inside of the house* in edited American prose, and British usage rejects the *of* outright. In the time sense, however, *inside of an hour* is an established American idiom that *inside an hour* only partly replaces, and *within an hour* is the neutral alternative. Bare *inside* with no object is an adverb: *Let's go inside.*

**1. Within the interior of (spatial; the *of* is dispensable).** He kept the receipts *inside of* a shoebox.
**2. In less than (a stated span of time; American idiom).** They had the road cleared *inside of* two hours.

1. He kept every receipt *inside of* a shoebox.
2. There was nothing *inside of* the envelope but a photograph.
3. Something *inside of* me knew it was a bad idea.
4. They had the road cleared *inside of* two hours.
5. The whole thing was over *inside of* a week.
6. You won't find a better price *inside of* fifty miles.

**See also:** [outside of](#outside-of), [in the middle of](#in-the-middle-of), [up to](#up-to), [in](01-core-in-on-at.md#in)

### instead of

**Pronunciation:** /ɪnˈstɛd əv/ &middot; casual /ɪnˈstɛdə/, /ˈstɛdə/ &middot; *in-STED uhv*
**Русский:** вместо — замена одного варианта другим; части должны быть грамматически параллельны
**Type:** complex &middot; **Register:** neutral — the everyday substitution preposition
**Complement:** noun phrase, gerund (*instead of driving*), or a nominal *wh*-clause (*instead of what you ordered*). **Never a bare infinitive** — *instead of to drive* is wrong — and **never a *that*-clause**. Note that it does take a full prepositional phrase in the parallel-contrast pattern: *We met at the office instead of at the diner.*
**Pattern:** [clause] *instead of* [noun phrase / verb-ing]. &middot; *Instead of* [noun phrase / verb-ing], [clause].
**Contrast:** *Instead of* is the preposition and needs an object; bare *instead* is an adverb and takes none — *We took the bus instead* is correct, *We took the bus instead of* is not. Against *in place of* and *in lieu of*, it is the plain, unrestricted choice and the only one that takes a gerund comfortably in speech. Against *rather than*, which can join two verbs directly (*rather than complain*), *instead of* must nominalize (*instead of complaining*).

Replaces one option with another. Both options must be grammatically parallel — the commonest error is a mismatch, as in *Instead of the bus, he walked*, where a noun is being contrasted with a verb.

1. Let's take the train *instead of* driving.
2. She ordered soup *instead of* the salad.
3. *Instead of* complaining, try filing a report.
4. He spent the afternoon reading *instead of* studying.
5. We met at the office *instead of* at the diner.
6. *Instead of* one long meeting, we'll have three short ones.

**See also:** [in place of](#in-place-of), [in lieu of](#in-lieu-of), [rather than](#rather-than), [as opposed to](#as-opposed-to), [in favor of](#in-favor-of)

### irrespective of

**Pronunciation:** /ˌɪrɪˈspɛktɪv əv/ &middot; connected speech /ˌɪrɪˈspɛktɪ və/ &middot; *ir-ih-SPEK-tiv uhv*
**Русский:** независимо от, безотносительно к — офиц.; фактор объявляется несущественным
**Type:** complex &middot; **Register:** formal
**Complement:** noun phrase, or a nominal wh-clause (*irrespective of what the survey shows*). **Never a *that*-clause.**
**Pattern:** [clause] *irrespective of* [noun phrase / wh-clause]. &middot; *Irrespective of* [noun phrase], [clause].
**Contrast:** Synonymous with *regardless of*, which is far more common in American English; *irrespective of* is the formal, legal-flavored twin. Note that *irrespective* is the correct word with the *ir-* prefix — the nonstandard *irregardless* is a blend of these two and should never be written.

Dismisses a factor as irrelevant to what the main clause asserts. It is the drafting word for non-discrimination clauses and eligibility rules.

1. The benefit is available to all employees *irrespective of* length of service.
2. *Irrespective of* cost, the repair has to be done this month.
3. Admission is open to applicants *irrespective of* nationality.
4. The rule applies *irrespective of* what the parties intended.
5. *Irrespective of* the outcome, the process was worth it.

**See also:** [regardless of](#regardless-of), [in spite of](#in-spite-of), [notwithstanding](12-marginal-participial.md#notwithstanding), [in the face of](#in-the-face-of)

### kind of

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkaɪnd əv/ &middot; weak /ˈkaɪn(d)ə/ — reduced in speech to *kinda*, as *sort of* reduces to *sorta* &middot; *KYND uhv*
**Русский:** род, разновидность (that kind of mistake); тж. разг. «вроде, как бы» — там не предлог
**Type:** complex classifier (*kind*, *sort*, *type* + *of*); the reduced degree adverb *kinda* is **not a preposition at all** &middot; **Register:** neutral as a classifier; the hedging use is informal and belongs to speech
**Complement:** **a bare noun, normally article-less** (*this kind of problem*, *that sort of thing*, *a new type of engine*). **Never a *that*-clause.** A gerund is fine where it names an activity (*the kind of thinking that gets people hurt*).
**Pattern:** [this / that / what] *kind of* [bare noun] &middot; [these / those] *kinds of* [plural noun] &middot; *a* [adjective] *kind of* [noun]
**Variants:** *sort of* and *type of* work identically; *sort of* is the commoner British choice, *kind of* the commoner American one, and *type of* is the most technical of the three.
**Contrast:** **Two different things wear these words, and only one of them takes an object.** In *that kind of mistake*, *kind of* governs a noun and classifies it. In *I'm kind of tired*, it governs **nothing** — it is a degree adverb hedging an adjective, and by the test this file applies throughout (no complement, therefore no preposition) it falls outside the class entirely; the same goes for *sort of* in *She sort of agreed*. The number trap belongs to the classifier: **make the demonstrative agree with *kind*, not with the noun** — *this kind of problem* and *these kinds of problems* are both right, while *these kind of problems* is the classic nonstandard blend. And *kind of a* (*that kind of a mistake*) is dialectal in American English and gets edited out.

In its prepositional-looking use, *kind of* sorts a noun into a category, and the unit is frozen: no article inside it, and the number of *kind* decided by the demonstrative in front of it. The homophonous hedge is listed here only so you can tell the two apart, since it takes no object and is therefore not a member of this class.

**1. (classifier) A category or variety of.** That *kind of* mistake is easy to make and hard to undo.
**2. (hedge, informal — takes no object, and so is not a preposition)** Somewhat, rather. I'm *kind of* tired.

1. That *kind of* mistake is easy to make and very hard to undo.
2. These *kinds of* disputes usually settle on the courthouse steps.
3. It's a new *type of* battery, not simply a bigger one.
4. What *sort of* documentation does the office actually need?
5. He is not the *kind of* manager who tells you what he thinks.
6. Compare the hedge, which takes no object at all: *I'm kind of tired.*

**See also:** [a lot of](#a-lot-of), [in the form of](#in-the-form-of), [such as](#such-as), [a matter of](07-agent-instrument-manner.md#a-matter-of), [in the way of](10-topic-reference-role.md#in-the-way-of)

### on account of

**Pronunciation:** /ɑn əˈkaʊnt əv/ &middot; connected speech /ɑn əˈkaʊn də/ &middot; *on uh-KOWNT uhv*
**Русский:** из-за, по причине — стилистически проще и разговорнее, чем because of
**Type:** complex &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal in standard use; the clause-taking version is regional and nonstandard
**Complement:** noun phrase, gerund (*on account of being sick*), or a nominal *wh*-clause (*on account of what he said*). **A *that*-clause or a bare finite clause after it — *on account of he was sick* — is nonstandard** in writing, though it is well attested in Southern and Appalachian speech and in dialogue.
**Pattern:** [clause] *on account of* [noun phrase / verb-ing]. &middot; *On account of* [noun phrase], [clause].
**Contrast:** A three-word *because of*, slightly more formal in writing and slightly folksier in speech, depending on the surrounding register. Against *due to*, it is safely adverbial. Note the unrelated literal phrase *on account* meaning "as partial payment" (*$50 on account*), which is accounting language, not this preposition.

Gives the reason for the main clause. In edited American prose *because of* is the default and *on account of* is a stylistic choice; in speech it often signals a homelier register.

1. The game was called *on account of* rain.
2. She retired early *on account of* her back.
3. *On account of* the holiday, the office closes at noon.
4. He was excused *on account of* being the only caregiver at home.
5. The road is closed *on account of* flooding.

**See also:** [because of](#because-of), [due to](#due-to), [owing to](#owing-to), [thanks to](#thanks-to), [in view of](#in-view-of)

### on behalf of

**Pronunciation:** /ɑn bɪˈhæf əv/ &middot; connected speech /ɑn bɪˈhæ və/ &middot; *on bih-HAF uhv*
**Русский:** от имени, по поручению — как представитель или агент кого-то
**Type:** complex &middot; **Register:** formal
**Complement:** noun phrase or objective-case pronoun, naming a person or body; **never a gerund and never a *that*-clause**. A free relative naming a person is fine (*on behalf of whoever ends up holding the lease*).
**Pattern:** [clause] *on behalf of* [noun phrase]. &middot; *On behalf of* [noun phrase], [clause].
**Variants:** *in behalf of* was traditionally distinguished in American usage — *on behalf of* = "as the agent of," *in behalf of* = "for the benefit of" — but the distinction has largely collapsed and *on behalf of* now covers both. *In behalf of* is safe to avoid.
**Contrast:** Against *for*, this specifies representation rather than mere benefit. Against *in the name of*, which invokes authority, *on behalf of* names a principal you are acting for. The reflexive form is *on my own behalf*, not *on behalf of myself*.

Marks the person or body the speaker or actor is representing. It is the standard opening of formal remarks and the standard signature line of counsel and agents.

1. *On behalf of* the entire staff, thank you for twenty years.
2. The attorney filed the motion *on behalf of* her client.
3. He accepted the award *on behalf of* his late father.
4. The union bargains *on behalf of* eleven thousand members.
5. *On behalf of* the mayor, I'd like to welcome you to the city.

**See also:** [in the name of](#in-the-name-of), [in charge of](#in-charge-of), [for the sake of](#for-the-sake-of), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)

### on the basis of

**Pronunciation:** /ɑn ðə ˈbeɪsɪs əv/ &middot; connected speech /ɑn ðə ˈbeɪsɪ sə/ &middot; *on thuh BAY-siss uhv*
**Русский:** на основании, исходя из — то, на чём построено решение или классификация
**Type:** complex &middot; **Register:** formal
**Complement:** noun phrase, or a nominal wh-clause (*on the basis of what he saw*). Never a *that*-clause.
**Pattern:** [clause] *on the basis of* [noun phrase]. &middot; *On the basis of* [noun phrase], [clause].
**Variants:** *on the basis that* is the clause-taking companion form and is a conjunction, not this preposition: *on the basis that the contract had lapsed*.
**Contrast:** Names the evidence or criterion a judgment rests on, where *because of* names a cause. *Hired on the basis of her portfolio* describes the grounds for a decision; *hired because of her portfolio* describes what made it happen. Non-discrimination law makes heavy use of *on the basis of* precisely because "grounds" is the legally relevant idea.

Identifies what a decision, claim, or classification was grounded in. It is the standard formula in statutes, methods sections, and administrative findings.

1. Applicants are ranked *on the basis of* test scores and interviews.
2. Discrimination *on the basis of* age is prohibited.
3. *On the basis of* the sample, the estimate is within two points.
4. The claim was denied *on the basis of* a paperwork error.
5. She was hired *on the basis of* what her references said, not her résumé.

**See also:** [by virtue of](#by-virtue-of), [according to](#according-to), [in light of](#in-light-of), [pursuant to](#pursuant-to)

### on the grounds of

**Pronunciation:** /ɑn ðə ˈɡraʊndz əv/ &middot; connected speech /ɑn ðə ˈɡraʊnd zə/ &middot; *on thuh GROWNDZ uhv*
**Русский:** на основании, по мотиву — заявленное основание решения, возражения или отказа
**Type:** complex &middot; **Register:** formal; legal and administrative
**Complement:** noun phrase naming a recognized ground — *age*, *race*, *national origin*, *cost*, *ill health*, *national security* — or a free relative (*on the grounds of what the inspector found*). **Never a *that*-clause, never a gerund.**
**Pattern:** [clause] *on the grounds of* [noun phrase] &middot; *refuse / object / dismiss / discriminate on the grounds of* [noun phrase]
**Variants:** *on grounds of*, without the article, is common in headlines and drafting; the singular *on the ground of* is older and now rare in American use. **The clause-taking companion is *on the grounds that* + a full clause** (*on the grounds that the notice was late*), which is a conjunction and not this preposition — the same split as *in case of* / *in case* and *on the basis of* / *on the basis that*.
**Contrast:** Exactly parallel to [*on the basis of*](#on-the-basis-of), and the two are near-interchangeable in discrimination law, but *on the grounds of* leans toward a reason given for refusing, objecting, or excluding, while *on the basis of* leans toward a criterion used for deciding: you are *hired on the basis of* your record and *dismissed on the grounds of* misconduct. Against *because of*, this one claims the reason was formally stated, not merely operative.

Names the stated reason for a decision, an objection, or a refusal. The reason is presented as one the speaker is prepared to defend, which is why the phrase belongs to rulings and appeals rather than to ordinary explanation.

1. The application was refused *on the grounds of* cost.
2. Discrimination *on the grounds of* age is unlawful in every state.
3. He objected *on the grounds of* relevance.
4. She retired early *on the grounds of* ill health.
5. The documents were withheld *on the grounds of* national security.
6. The appeal succeeded *on the grounds of* what the trial transcript actually showed.

**See also:** [on the basis of](#on-the-basis-of), [because of](#because-of), [by virtue of](#by-virtue-of), [in view of](#in-view-of), [on the grounds of](06-cause-purpose.md#on-the-grounds-of)

### on the heels of

**Pronunciation:** /ɑn ðə ˈhilz əv/ &middot; connected speech /ɑn ðə ˈhil zə/ &middot; *on thuh HEELZ uhv*
**Русский:** сразу вслед за, по пятам — событие, наступившее вплотную за другим
**Type:** complex &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal; a standard idiom of news writing
**Complement:** noun phrase naming an event; a gerund is possible but uncommon (*on the heels of losing the contract*). **Never a *that*-clause.** A free relative naming the event is fine (*on the heels of what happened in March*).
**Pattern:** *come/follow/arrive on the heels of* [noun phrase] &middot; [clause], *on the heels of* [noun phrase] &middot; *hard/hot on the heels of* [noun phrase]
**Variants:** *hard on the heels of* and *hot on the heels of* are the intensified forms and mean "immediately after." *Heels* is invariable — there is no *on the heel of*.
**Contrast:** Against [*in the wake of*](#in-the-wake-of), the phrase it is most often confused with, **this one claims closeness in time and nothing more**: *in the wake of the scandal* says the scandal caused what followed, while *on the heels of the scandal* says only that the second thing came fast behind the first. Reach for *in the wake of* when you mean consequence and *on the heels of* when you mean the very next thing. Against plain *after*, both add speed and neither is neutral.

Marks an event as following hard upon another. The metaphor is pursuit, so the two events have to be close enough together for the second to be treading on the first.

1. The layoffs came *on the heels of* a record quarter.
2. *On the heels of* the recall, two more suppliers suspended shipments.
3. The announcement arrived hard *on the heels of* the mayor's resignation.
4. A second storm followed *on the heels of* the first.
5. *On the heels of* losing the contract, the firm closed its Denver office.
6. The bill passed *on the heels of* three years of failed attempts.

**See also:** [in the wake of](#in-the-wake-of), [as a result of](#as-a-result-of), [subsequent to](#subsequent-to), [in advance of](#in-advance-of), [following](12-marginal-participial.md#following)

### on the lookout for

**Pronunciation:** /ɑn ðə ˈlʊkaʊt fɔr/ &middot; weak /ən ðə ˈlʊkaʊt fər/ — *on* reduces to /ən/ &middot; *on thuh LOOK-owt for*
**Русский:** высматривая, начеку насчёт — следит за чем-то, будучи занят другим
**Type:** complex &middot; **Register:** informal to neutral; conversation, journalism, and police bulletins
**Complement:** noun phrase. **Never a *that*-clause and never a gerund** — *on the lookout for hiring someone* is wrong; write *on the lookout for a new hire*, or switch to *looking to hire*.
**Pattern:** *be/keep/stay on the lookout for* [noun phrase]
**Contrast:** **This member of the *on the ___* frame closes with *for*, not *of***, and *on the lookout of* is simply an error. Against [*in search of*](#in-search-of), which implies active hunting, *on the lookout for* implies watchful readiness while doing something else. The object may be wanted (*on the lookout for a bargain*) or unwanted (*on the lookout for signs of infection*), which is where it parts from [*in the market for*](#in-the-market-for), whose object is always wanted. **Bare *on the lookout* takes no object** (*Stay on the lookout*).

States that the subject is watching for something while otherwise occupied. The noun *lookout* is a fossil of the naval watch, surviving in this phrase, in *keep a lookout*, and in the person posted as one.

1. Be *on the lookout for* a silver pickup with a cracked windshield.
2. Nurses are *on the lookout for* early signs of infection.
3. She's always *on the lookout for* a first edition of that book.
4. Editors should be *on the lookout for* the same three errors in every draft.
5. Stay *on the lookout for* the turnoff; it isn't marked from this direction.
6. The bulletin asks agencies to be *on the lookout for* the vehicle.

**See also:** [in search of](#in-search-of), [in the market for](#in-the-market-for), [in line for](#in-line-for), [on the verge of](#on-the-verge-of), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)

### on the part of

**Pronunciation:** /ɑn ðə ˈpɑrt əv/ &middot; connected speech /ɑn ðə ˈpɑr də/ &middot; *on thuh PART uhv*
**Русский:** со стороны (кого-то) — приписывает отвлечённое поведение ответственному лицу
**Type:** complex &middot; **Register:** formal; legal, academic, and report writing
**Complement:** noun phrase or objective-case pronoun naming a person or a body — **never a thing**. **Never a *that*-clause, never a *wh*-clause, never a gerund.**
**Pattern:** [noun of conduct or attitude] *on the part of* [person or body] &middot; *There was no* [noun] *on the part of* [person].
**Contrast:** Its one job is to attach an actor to an abstract noun of behavior — *negligence*, *reluctance*, *failure*, *bad faith*, *misunderstanding* — where the nominalization has lost the subject a verb would have carried: *negligence on the part of the contractor* is *the contractor was negligent*, kept nominal. That is also its weakness, since nine times in ten a *by* phrase or a plain verb is shorter. **The complement must be animate** — *a failure on the part of the software* is a misuse for *a failure of the software*. Do not confuse it with *for my part* ("as far as I'm concerned") or *take the part of* ("side with"), which are separate fixed phrases.

Assigns an abstract behavior to the person or body responsible for it. It exists because English nominalizations drop their subjects, and this is the phrase that puts one back.

1. There was no bad faith *on the part of* either side.
2. The report found negligence *on the part of* two supervisors.
3. Some reluctance *on the part of* the family is understandable.
4. It was a genuine misunderstanding *on the part of* the clerk.
5. Any failure *on the part of* the tenant to give notice voids the clause.
6. The delay reflects real indecision *on the part of* the board.

**See also:** [on behalf of](#on-behalf-of), [in the name of](#in-the-name-of), [by virtue of](#by-virtue-of), [on the part of](07-agent-instrument-manner.md#on-the-part-of), [by](02-core-relational.md#by)

### on the receiving end of

**Pronunciation:** /ɑn ðə rɪˈsivɪŋ ˈɛnd əv/ &middot; weak /ən ðə rəˈsivɪŋ ˈɛn də/ &middot; *on thuh rih-SEE-ving END uhv*
**Русский:** на принимающей стороне, тот, на ком это отыгралось — с оттенком сочувствия
**Type:** complex &middot; **Register:** informal to neutral; conversation and journalism
**Complement:** noun phrase, almost always naming something unpleasant — a lawsuit, a tirade, a beating, a joke. **Never a *that*-clause and never a gerund.**
**Pattern:** *be/find oneself on the receiving end of* [noun phrase]
**Contrast:** **The phrase is negatively loaded by default**: *on the receiving end of* a raise sounds wrong, while *on the receiving end of* a lecture sounds exactly right, and a positive object is heard as irony. Against plain *receive*, it moves the subject into the patient's position and quietly asks for sympathy. Against [*at the hands of*](#at-the-hands-of), which is graver and names the **agent**, this one names the **act**. The mirror phrase *on the giving end* exists but is far rarer and usually appears only to complete the contrast.

Puts the subject in the position of the one to whom something is done. It is a sympathy device: the grammar makes the subject an active noun while the meaning makes it a patient.

1. Small suppliers are usually *on the receiving end of* these renegotiations.
2. He has been *on the receiving end of* two lawsuits from the same tenant.
3. Nobody wants to be *on the receiving end of* one of her performance reviews.
4. Referees are *on the receiving end of* abuse for ninety minutes a week.
5. She found herself *on the receiving end of* a very public apology.
6. The town was *on the receiving end of* every storm that came up the valley.

**See also:** [at the hands of](#at-the-hands-of), [at the mercy of](#at-the-mercy-of), [in the face of](#in-the-face-of), [on the part of](#on-the-part-of), [subject to](#subject-to)

### on the strength of

**Pronunciation:** /ɑn ðə ˈstrɛŋkθ əv/ &middot; also /ˈstrɛŋθ/ &middot; connected speech /ɑn ðə ˈstrɛŋk θə/ &middot; *on thuh STRENGKTH uhv*
**Русский:** на основании, опираясь на — довод или заслуга, решившие дело
**Type:** complex &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** noun phrase, or a free relative (*on the strength of what the scouts reported*). **Never a *that*-clause, never a gerund.**
**Pattern:** [clause] *on the strength of* [noun phrase]. &middot; *On the strength of* [noun phrase], [clause].
**Contrast:** Against [*on the basis of*](#on-the-basis-of), which names a criterion neutrally, *on the strength of* adds that the thing named was **persuasive enough to carry the decision**: *hired on the basis of her portfolio* says the portfolio was what they looked at, *hired on the strength of her portfolio* says it was what convinced them. Against *because of*, it confines the cause to evidence, testimony, or a record that somebody actually weighed. It resists a weak object — *on the strength of a rumor* reads as deliberate irony, and that irony is the proof of the rule.

Names the evidence or achievement that carried a decision. It presupposes that somebody was persuaded, which is why it attaches to hirings, loans, verdicts, promotions, and signings.

1. The loan was approved *on the strength of* two years of receipts.
2. She was promoted *on the strength of* a single quarter.
3. *On the strength of* his testimony, the charges were refiled.
4. The band was signed *on the strength of* a four-song demo.
5. The town rebuilt its budget *on the strength of* what the new plant paid in taxes.
6. He got the fellowship *on the strength of* one unpublished chapter.

**See also:** [on the basis of](#on-the-basis-of), [by virtue of](#by-virtue-of), [in light of](#in-light-of), [because of](#because-of), [on the strength of](06-cause-purpose.md#on-the-strength-of)

### on the verge of

**Pronunciation:** /ɑn ðə ˈvɜrdʒ əv/ &middot; connected speech /ɑn ðə ˈvɜr dʒə/ &middot; *on thuh VURJ uhv*
**Русский:** на грани, вот-вот — в шаге от перемены состояния, которая может и не произойти
**Type:** complex &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **noun phrase or gerund** (*on the verge of collapse*, *on the verge of collapsing*). Because *of* is a preposition, ***on the verge of to collapse* is wrong**, and so is ***on the verge to collapse***. **Never a *that*-clause, never a *wh*-clause.**
**Pattern:** *be on the verge of* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *bring/push someone to the verge of* [noun phrase]
**Variants:** *on the brink of* is the near-twin and is preferred for large disasters (*on the brink of war*); *on the point of* is the more formal, British-leaning version and takes the same gerund. **The *of* is not optional** — the elliptical *She was on the verge* survives only where the complement has just been said.
**Contrast:** Against sense 3 of [*close to*](#close-to) (*close to tears*), which is gradable and vague about the outcome, *on the verge of* names a **threshold about to be crossed**, and it takes only outcomes, never places: *close to the airport* is fine, *on the verge of the airport* is nonsense. Against [*in danger of*](#in-danger-of), it is neutral about whether the outcome is welcome — you can be *on the verge of* a breakthrough as easily as a breakdown, where *in danger of* is always bad.

Places the subject one step short of a change of state. Because the change has not happened, the phrase stays true even if it never does.

1. The company was *on the verge of* bankruptcy when the order came in.
2. She was *on the verge of* tears and would not look up.
3. Researchers say they are *on the verge of* a usable test.
4. He was *on the verge of* quitting when the transfer came through.
5. Two of the three species are *on the verge of* disappearing from the river.
6. The talks are *on the verge of* collapse for the third time this year.

**See also:** [close to](#close-to), [in danger of](#in-danger-of), [short of](#short-of), [in the process of](#in-the-process-of), [up against](#up-against)

### on top of

**Pronunciation:** /ɑn ˈtɑp əv/ &middot; casual /ɑnˈtɑpə/ &middot; *on TOP uhv*
**Русский:** на, поверх; тж. вдобавок к (обычно обременительно); тж. держащий под полным контролем
**Type:** complex &middot; **Register:** neutral; sense 3 informal
**Complement:** noun phrase, objective-case pronoun, or gerund in sense 2 (*on top of paying rent*). Sense 3 also takes a nominal *wh*-clause (*on top of what the auditors want*). **Never a *that*-clause.**
**Pattern:** [clause] *on top of* [noun phrase] &middot; *On top of* [noun phrase], [clause]. &middot; *right on top of* [noun phrase]
**Contrast:** Sense 1 is literal and takes intensifiers (*right on top of*); senses 2 and 3 are frozen idioms. Against *in addition to*, sense 2 is the informal equivalent and usually carries a complaint — things pile *on top of* each other. Against *above*, sense 1 requires contact: a lamp *above* the table is not touching it; a book *on top of* the table is. **Bare *on top* is an adverb, not this preposition**, and takes no object: *The cake had berries on top* is complete; *on top of the cake* needs its object.

**1. Resting on the upper surface of.** The keys are *on top of* the refrigerator.
**2. In addition to (usually burdensome).** *On top of* everything else, the car wouldn't start.
**3. In full control of; up to date with.** She stays *on top of* every deadline.

1. The keys are *on top of* the refrigerator.
2. The truck stopped right *on top of* the crosswalk.
3. Stack the folders *on top of* the printer for now.
4. *On top of* everything else, the furnace quit.
5. *On top of* paying rent, they cover their own utilities.
6. She stays *on top of* every deadline without visible effort.
7. The manager is completely *on top of* the situation.

**See also:** [in addition to](#in-addition-to), [as well as](#as-well-as), [in front of](#in-front-of), [in charge of](#in-charge-of), [on](01-core-in-on-at.md#on)

### out of

**Pronunciation:** /ˈaʊt əv/ &middot; casual /ˈaʊtə/, spelled *outta* &middot; *OWT uhv*
**Русский:** из (изнутри, из источника, из материала); из числа; без (кончилось); из (побуждения); вне
**Type:** complex &middot; **Register:** neutral — one of the most frequent prepositions in the language
**Complement:** noun phrase or objective-case pronoun; a gerund in sense 5 only (*out of not wanting to offend anyone*). In senses 4 and 5 the complement is characteristically a bare mass or abstract noun (*out of milk*, *out of curiosity*). **Never a *that*-clause**; a nominal *wh*-clause is possible in sense 2 (*out of what little was left*).
**Pattern:** [verb of motion] *out of* [noun phrase] &middot; *be out of* [noun phrase] &middot; [number] *out of* [number] &middot; *made out of* [material]
**Contrast:** *Out* alone is an adverb or a verb particle and takes no object (*She went out*, *Cross it out*); *out of* is the preposition and requires one. Against *from*, sense 1 insists on prior enclosure — you come *out of* a building but *from* a city. Against *of*, sense 3 (*made out of wood*) stresses transformation of raw material, where *made of wood* just names the substance.

**1. Moving from inside to outside.** She walked *out of* the room without a word.
**2. From (a source, origin, or material).** The whole sculpture is carved *out of* a single log.
**3. From a set of (with numbers).** Three *out of* four dentists said the same thing.
**4. No longer having; lacking.** We're *out of* coffee.
**5. From (a motive).** He apologized *out of* habit rather than conviction.
**6. Beyond the limits of (a range or state).** The plane was already *out of* sight.
**7. Absent from (a place the subject belongs to or is expected at).** She's *out of* the office until Monday.

1. She walked *out of* the room without a word.
2. Get the dog *out of* the kitchen.
3. The whole sculpture is carved *out of* a single log.
4. He built the shed *out of* salvaged lumber.
5. Three *out of* four respondents said they would pay more.
6. She scored 92 *out of* 100.
7. We're *out of* coffee and nearly *out of* milk.
8. The team is *out of* contention for the first time in years.
9. He apologized *out of* habit rather than conviction.
10. I only asked *out of* curiosity.
11. The plane was already *out of* sight.
12. Your call is *out of* the ordinary, and so is the answer.
13. She made supper *out of* what little was left in the pantry.
14. He said nothing, *out of* not wanting to make it worse.
15. She's *out of* the office until Monday and unreachable before that.
16. He has been *out of* the country since June.

**See also:** [outside of](#outside-of), [inside of](#inside-of), [away from](#away-from), [short of](#short-of), [of](02-core-relational.md#of), [from](02-core-relational.md#from)

### outside of

**Pronunciation:** /aʊtˈsaɪd əv/ &middot; connected speech /aʊtˈsaɪ də/ &middot; *owt-SYDE uhv*
**Русский:** за пределами, вне (of необязательно); тж. амер. разг. кроме, если не считать
**Type:** complex &middot; **Register:** neutral in sense 1 (with the *of* often edited out); informal and **chiefly American** in sense 2
**Complement:** noun phrase, gerund (sense 2: *outside of going to the store*), or a nominal *wh*-clause (*outside of what has already been said*). **Never a *that*-clause.**
**Pattern:** [clause] *outside of* [noun phrase] &middot; *Outside of* [noun phrase], [clause].
**Contrast:** **In the spatial sense the *of* is optional** and edited American prose usually drops it — *outside the building*, not *outside of the building*. Sense 2, meaning "except for," is an Americanism that many editors also cut in favor of *aside from* or *except for*, but it is fully idiomatic in speech. Bare *outside* is an adverb: *Wait outside.*

**1. Beyond the limits of (spatial or figurative; the *of* is dispensable).** The plant is *outside of* the city limits.
**2. Except for; apart from (American, informal).** *Outside of* a sore shoulder, I feel fine.

1. The plant is just *outside of* the city limits.
